`photostim_stop_times`, and trial-end events.\n    80→- `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries`: continuous tracked markers including `Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, `Camera0_side_JawTracking`, and `Camera0_side_NoseTracking`.\n    81→- Sample file inspection (`sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_behavior+ecephys+ogen.nwb`):\n    82→- 368 trials, 1952 units.\n    83→- `trial_instruction` values are strings like `right`.\n    84→- `early_lick` values are strings like `early` / `no early`.\n    85→- `outcome` values are strings like `hit` / `ignore`.\n    86→- `photostim_*` trial-table fields are strings such as `N/A` on no-stim trials.\n    87→- Tongue tracking is a continuous `(n_frames, 3)` time series with columns `(tongue_x, tongue_y, tongue_likelihood)` and explicit timestamps.\n    88→- Units include ragged `spike_times` plus per-unit CCF annotation in `anno_name`.\n    89→\n    90→### Dataset Size (from data files)\n    91→| Statistic | Value |\n    92→|-----------|-------|\n “This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2.” |\n   121→| QC classifier performance | ROC AUC > 0.9; false alarm rates 7.8/6.4/7.3/5.5/4.3% by major area | White paper: “Ten-fold crossvalidation demonstrated that the AUC was on average > 0.9” and datapaper false alarm-rate text |\n   122→\n   123→\n   124→### Processing Details\n   125→- Task structure from `methods.txt` / datapaper:\n   126→- sample epoch: three pure tones (150 ms each) with 100 ms inter-tone intervals,\n   127→- delay epoch: 1.2 s,\n   128→- go cue: 0.1 s auditory cue marking delay end,\n   129→- response epoch: 1.5 s answer period,\n   130→- consumption period: 1.5 s after correct lick.\n   131→- Early licks during sample/delay trigger replay of the epoch in the task, and early-lick plus no-response trials are excluded for analysis.\n   132→- Published analyses align task epochs to go cue (`Time to go (s)` in figures), and reference code treats spike times as already go-cue referenced.\n   133→- Video tracking uses DeepLabCut markers for tongue, jaw, and nose at 300 Hz.\n   134→- Published population choice decoding uses causal 200 ms windows stepped every 10 ms; our conversion must deviate here only because the task specification requires 50 ms bins over [-2.5, 1.5] s.\n   135→\n   136→### Curation Steps\n   137→\n   138→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   139→- Kilosort2 output was quality-controlled with 15 metrics.\n   140→- Five region-specific logistic-regression classifiers were trained from manually curated `good` versus `unlabeled` units.\n   141→- Area mapping for classifiers:\n   142→- cortex classifier: ALM, other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory, cortical subplate,\n   143→- striatum classifier: striatum and pallidum,\n   144→- thalamus classifier: thalamus and hypothalamus,\n   145→- midbrain classifier: midbrain and pons,\n   146→- medulla classifier: medulla and cerebellum.\n   147→- False alarm rates on held-out data: cortex 7.8%, striatum 6.4%, thalamus 7.3%, midbrain 5.5%, medulla 4.3%.\n   148→\n   149→**Trial curation rules**:\n   150→- Exclude early-lick trials.\n   151→- Exclude no-response trials.\n   152→- For overall performance in the paper, use control trials only (no photostimulation) and exclude early licks.\n   153→- For photostimulation-effect analyses, require at least 10 photostimulation trials per unit.\n   154→\n   155→### Decoders Trained\n   156→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   157→| Choice (published population decoder, Figure 6D) | Above chance (> 0.5) with 200-neuron pseudo-populations; exact numeric trace shown in figure, not tabulated in text |\n   158→| QC classifier (white paper, not behavior decoder) | ROC AUC > 0.9 on average across major-area classifiers |\n   159→\n   160→---\n   161→\n   162→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   163→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   164→\n   165→### Discrepancies Found\n   166→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n   167→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   168→| Session count | Reference preprocessing code groups and processes all sessions it finds; downstream analyses operate on QC-filtered/preprocessed sessions | 174 raw NWB session files | 173 behavioral sessions after stringent QC | One raw NWB session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508_behavior+ecephys+ogen.nwb`) has zero units with `classification == good`; after excluding sessions with zero good units, raw data and papers agree at 173 analyzable sessions. |\n   169→| Neuron count | Code relies on classifier-selected good units from QC files | 272,227 raw units across NWB files; 69,453 units with `classification == good` in NWB | 69,943 good units | Use classifier-approved units as the intended population. The NWB `classification == good` total is close but 490 below the paper total; likely due to release/version differences or minor pipeline differences between paper and NWB export. Track this in later sanity checks. |\n   170→| Trials/session | Raw NWB retains all task trials | 545.9 trials/session mean raw | 476 trials/session mean for selected experimental sessions | The papers’ trial statistic reflects analyzed sessions/trials after exclusions; raw NWB stores all trials. Conversion should keep raw trial availability first, then apply explicit trial filters required by the decoder/reference logic. |\n   171→| Session behavioral filter | Reference text states session inclusion by performance and >=50 correct left/right trials | Applying a simple raw-NWB interpretation of that rule yields 152 sessions, not 173 | 173 sessions reported overall | The published 173-session count is therefore not explained solely by a naive behavioral filter on NWB trial-table labels. For conversion, do not use this raw behavioral filter as the primary session-selection rule. Prefer the QC-consistent rule of excluding sessions with zero good units, then retain trial-level metadata for later filtering. |\n   172→| Binning/alignment | Reference preprocessing code bins spikes with `bw=0.04 s`, `stride=0.0034 s`, aligned to go cue | Raw NWB contains spike times plus go-cue and behavioral timestamps | Published choice decoder uses causal 200 ms windows, 10 ms step, aligned to go cue | Preserve the core reference principle of go-cue alignment and classifier-based unit curation, but deliberately re-bin to 50 ms non-overlapping bins over [-2.5, 1.5] s because that is the explicit decoder-task requirement. |\n   173→| Trial filtering | Reference code’s `get_regular_trial_mask` excludes early-lick, auto-water, free-water, no-response, and stimulation trials for specific downstream analyses | Raw NWB exposes these fields directly in the trial table | Papers exclude early-lick and no-response trials for analysis; control-trial metrics exclude photostim | Distinguish between raw trial retention and analysis-specific masks. Build the converted dataset from raw trials with required metadata, but document and implement any exclusions that are necessary for alignment validity, QC, or decoder robustness. |\n   174→\n   175→---\n   176→\n   177→## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   178→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   179→\n   180→### Variable Mapping\n   181→| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n   182→|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n   183→| `units/spike_times` for units with `classification == good` | `neural` | Bin spikes into firing rates (Hz) in non-overlapping 50 ms bins over [-2.5, 1.5) s relative to go cue | `sliding_histogram`, `process_one_area` | Keep sessions with at least 1 good unit; final session inclusion target is 173 sessions after excluding the zero-good-unit session. |\n   184→| `units/is_good_trials` plus `units/obs_intervals` | `neural` validity mask during construction | Keep only trials whose full `[-2.5, +1.5] s` go-aligned window lies inside the union of good-unit observation intervals. When the selected trial count matches the NWB `is_good_trials` columns, use `is_good_trials` directly; otherwise fall back to raw `obs_intervals` support. | No direct code equivalent in explored reference code; NWB-specific validity fields | This resolved a discovered bug where ephys-backed trials were not always a simple prefix of the behavioral trial table. |\n   185→| `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/go_start_times.timestamps` | temporal alignment anchor | Per-trial go cue absolute timestamp; subtract from neural/behavioral timestamps | Reference code treats spike times as go-cue aligned | One go cue per trial. |\n   186→| Earliest `sample_start_times` event within trial window | `input[0]` (`time_from_tone_onset_s`) | For each bin, set value to `(bin_center_rel_go - sample_onset_rel_go)` in seconds | Raw-task timing consistent with `sample_time` use in preprocessing code | Normal trials give ~-1.85 s tone onset; early-lick trials can have earlier tone onset because replay extends trial structure. |\n   187→| `photostim_onset`, `photostim_duration`, `photostim_power` trial-table columns | `input[1]` (`photostim_on`) | Binary time series: 1 during `[onset, onset+duration)` relative to go cue, else 0 | Reference code keeps `stimulation` per trial; papers say photostim ends before go cue | Trial-table onset/duration matched the event-series timestamps in spot checks. |\n   188→| First post-go lick direction inferred from left/right lick events | `output[0]` (`choice`) | Encode left=0, right=1 | Paper’s choice variable is actual lick direction on responded trials | Primary rule: first post-go lick. Fallback 1: first lick anywhere in trial. Fallback 2: trial instruction for no-lick ignore trials. This fallback is required because ignore trials have no post-go lick but the decoder spec still requires a binary choice output. |\n   189→| `trials/outcome` | `output[1]` (`outcome`) | Map `ignore -> 0`, `miss -> 1`, `hit -> 2` | Consistent with paper’s hit/error/no-response categories | Use exact trial-table labels. |\n   190→| `trials/early_lick` | `output[2]` (`early_lick`) | Map `no early -> 0`, `early -> 1` | Consistent with reference code’s `early_lick_trials` mask | Keep all trials; do not exclude early trials because early lick itself is a decoder target. |\n   191→| `BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking[:,1]` with timestamps | `output[3]` (`tongue_y_bin`) | Align absolute timestamps to go cue; within each 50 ms bin take the last available `tongue_y` sample; discretize within session using 40th and 60th percentiles over all aligned binned values | `align_markers_between_lims` | Reference marker alignment uses the last frame within each time step rather than averaging. Use `tongue_likelihood` only for QC diagnostics, not thresholding, to stay close to reference code. |\n   192→| `subject.subject_id` or folder name `sub-<id>` | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Deduplicate subjects; map each session to subject index | N/A | Subject order follows converted session order. |\n   193→| `units/anno_name` | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Use exact non-empty CCF annotation strings for each kept unit | Reference code carries exact `ccf_label` strings | Exact annotations are closer to the raw NWB + reference code than imposing a new coarse atlas grouping. |\n   194→\n   195→### Key Decisions\n   196→1. **Session inclusion**: Keep sessions with at least one `classification == good` unit and at least two trials. This matches the raw-to-paper session discrepancy best: 174 raw NWB files become 173 analyzable sessions once the one zero-good-unit session is removed.\n   197→2. **Neuron inclusion**: Use units with `classification == good` as the primary QC filter, because the papers and code describe classifier-selected good units as the analysis set.\n   198→3. **Brain-region labels**: Use exact `anno_name` strings for `brain_regions` rather than collapsing to 14 coarse groups. This is the least lossy mapping and matches the reference code’s use of exact `ccf_label` values.\n   199→4. **Neural binning**: Use 50 ms non-overlapping bins and convert to firing rates in Hz. This intentionally departs from the paper’s 40 ms / 3.4 ms preprocessing and 200 ms / 10 ms choice-decoder windows only because the user’s decoder task explicitly requires 50 ms bins.\n   200→5. **Tone-onset input**: Use the earliest sample-start event in each trial as tone onset. This preserves replay-induced timing shifts visible in early-lick trials and is the most faithful raw-data representation of “time from tone onset”.\n   201→6. **Photostim input**: Represent photostimulation as a time-varying binary series, not a static trial label, because the decoder task requests the on/off state at every time point.\n   202→7. **Choice output edge case**: For hit/miss trials, decode actual lick direction from lick-event timing. For ignore trials with no lick, use the fallback hierarchy documented above. This is the only unavoidable ambiguity created by the target spec relative to the raw data.\n   203→8. **Trial retention and alignment validity**: Retain early-lick, miss, hit, ignore, and photostimulation trials because these are either decoder outputs or decoder inputs. Do not apply the reference code’s `regular_trial_mask` wholesale. However, do require that the full neural decoding window is actually supported by the raw session observation interval.\n   204→9. **NWB trial mapping rule**: Do not assume `units/is_good_trials.shape[1]` means “use the first N behavioral trials”. In several sessions the ephys-backed trials form an offset block relative to the full behavioral trial table; use raw go times plus `obs_intervals` to select valid trials.\n   205→10. **Tongue discretization**: Discretize after session-level alignment and binning, using percentiles over the kept binned tongue-y values from that session, exactly as requested by the decoder task.\n   206→\n [ ] For random trials, compare converted photostim binary vectors against raw trial-table onset/duration and against behavioral-event photostim timestamps.\n   210→- [ ] For random trials, compare There is no dF/F step in the explored reference path.\n- Raw spike times are treated as already aligned to go cue in `process_one_area` (“in Susu's data, spike_times are relative to go cue time”).\n- The preprocessing script used in the paper repository (`code/Sherlock/preprocess_all_ephys.py`) sets `bw=0.04`, `stride=0.0034`, `begin_time=-3.`, `end_time=3.`, and `qc_mode='classifier'`.\n- Neuron curation is required. good neuron ids from the QC file,\n- splitting left/right hemisphere by CCF ML coordinate,\n- then carrying forward unit QC metrics such as `presence_ratio`, `amplitude_cutoff`, `isi_violation`, `start_time` and `stop_time`.\n- `units`: unit table with spike times and quality metrics including `unit_quality`, `avg_firing_rate`, `presence_ratio`, `amplitude_cutoff`, `isi_violation`, `photostim_start_times`, `photostim_stop_times`, and trial-end events.\n- `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries`: continuous tracked markers including `Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, `Camera0_side_JawTracking`, and `Camera0_side_NoseTracking`.\n- Sample file inspection (`sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_behavior+ecephys+ogen.nwb`):\n- 368 trials, 1952 units.\n- `trial_instruction` values are strings like `right`.\n- `early_lick` values are strings like `early` / `no early`.\n- `outcome` values are strings like `hit` / `ignore`.\n- `photostim_*` trial-table fields are strings such as `N/A` on no-stim trials.\n- Tongue tracking is a continuous `(n_frames, 3)` time series with columns `(tongue_x, tongue_y, tongue_likelihood)` and explicit timestamps.\n- Units include ragged ms inter-tone intervals,\n- delay epoch: 1.2 s,\n- go cue: 0.1 s auditory cue marking delay end,\n- response epoch: 1.5 s answer period,\n- consumption period: 1.5 s after correct lick.\n- Early licks during sample/delay trigger replay of the epoch in the task, and early-lick plus no-response trials are excluded for analysis.\n- Published analyses align task epochs to go cue (`Time to go (s)` in figures), and reference code treats spike times as already go-cue referenced.\n- Video tracking uses DeepLabCut markers for tongue, jaw, and nose at 300 Hz.\n- Published population choice decoding uses causal 200 ms windows stepped every 10 ms; our conversion must deviate here only because the task specification requires 50 ms bins over [-2.5, 1.5] s.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Kilosort2 output was quality-controlled with 15 metrics.\n- Five region-specific logistic-regression classifiers were trained from manually curated `good` versus `unlabeled` units.\n- Area mapping for classifiers:\n- cortex classifier: ALM, other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory, cortical subplate,\n- striatum classifier: striatum and pallidum,\n- thalamus classifier: thalamus and hypothalamus,\n- midbrain classifier: midbrain and pons,\n- medulla classifier: medulla and cerebellum.\n- False alarm rates on held-out data: cortex 7.8%, striatum 6.4%, thalamus 7.3%, midbrain 5.5%, medulla 4.3%.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- Exclude early-lick trials.\n- Exclude no-response trials.\n- For overall performance in the paper, use control trials only (no photostimulation) and exclude early licks.\n- For photostimulation-effect analyses, require at least 10 photostimulation trials per unit.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| Choice (published population decoder, Figure 6D) | Above chance (> 0.5) with 200-neuron pseudo-populations; exact numeric trace shown in figure, not tabulated in text |\n| QC classifier (white paper, not behavior decoder) | ROC AUC > 0.9 on average across major-area classifiers |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Discrepancies Found\n| Topic | Code says | Data shows | When the selected trial count matches the NWB `is_good_trials` columns, use `is_good_trials` directly; otherwise fall back to raw `obs_intervals` support. | No direct code equivalent in explored reference code; NWB-specific validity fields | This resolved a discovered bug where ephys-backed trials were not always a simple prefix of the behavioral trial table. |\n| `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/go_start_times.timestamps` | temporal alignment anchor | Per-trial go cue absolute timestamp; subtract from neural/behavioral timestamps | Reference code treats spike times as go-cue aligned | One go cue per trial. |\n| Earliest `sample_start_times` event within trial window | `input[0]` (`time_from_tone_onset_s`) | For each bin, set value to `(bin_center_rel_go - sample_onset_rel_go)` in seconds | Raw-task timing consistent with `sample_time` use in preprocessing code | Normal trials give ~-1.85 s tone onset; early-lick trials can have earlier tone onset because replay extends trial structure. |\n| `photostim_onset`, `photostim_duration`, `photostim_power` trial-table columns | `input[1]` (`photostim_on`) | Binary time series: 1 during `[onset, onset+duration)` relative to go cue, else 0 | Reference code keeps `stimulation` per trial; papers say photostim ends before go cue | Trial-table onset/duration matched the event-series timestamps in spot checks. |\n| First post-go lick direction inferred from left/right lick events | `output[0]` (`choice`) | Encode left=0, right=1 | Paper’s choice variable is actual lick direction on responded trials | Primary rule: first post-go lick. Fallback 1: first lick anywhere in trial. Fallback 2: trial instruction for no-lick ignore trials. This fallback is required because ignore trials have no post-go lick but the decoder spec still requires a binary choice output. |\n| `trials/outcome` | `output[1]` (`outcome`) | Map `ignore -> 0`, `miss -> 1`, `hit -> 2` | Consistent with paper’s hit/error/no-response categories | Use exact trial-table labels. |\n| `trials/early_lick` | `output[2]` (`early_lick`) | Map `no early -> 0`, `early -> 1` | Consistent with reference code’s `early_lick_trials` mask | Keep all trials; do not exclude early trials because early lick itself is a decoder target. |\n| `BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking[:,1]` with timestamps | `output[3]` (`tongue_y_bin`) | Align absolute timestamps to go cue; within each 50 ms bin take the last available `tongue_y` sample; discretize within session using 40th and 60th percentiles over all aligned binned values | `align_markers_between_lims` | Reference marker alignment uses the last frame within each time step rather than averaging. Use `tongue_likelihood` only for QC diagnostics, not thresholding, to stay close to reference code. |\n| `subject.subject_id` or folder name `sub-<id>` | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Deduplicate subjects; map each session to subject index | N/A | Subject order follows converted session order. |\n| `units/anno_name` | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Use exact non-empty CCF annotation strings for each kept unit | Reference code carries exact `ccf_label` strings | Exact annotations are closer to the raw NWB + reference code than imposing a new coarse atlas grouping. |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Session inclusion**: Keep sessions with at least one `classification == good` unit and at least two trials. This matches the raw-to-paper session discrepancy best: 174 raw NWB files become 173 analyzable sessions once the one zero-good-unit session is removed.\n2. **Neuron inclusion**: Use units with `classification == good` as the primary QC filter, because the papers and code describe classifier-selected good units as the analysis set.\n3. **Brain-region labels**: Use exact `anno_name` strings for `brain_regions` rather than collapsing to 14 coarse groups. This is the least lossy mapping and matches the reference code’s use of exact `ccf_label` values.\n4. **Neural binning**: Use 50 ms non-overlapping bins and convert to firing rates in Hz. This intentionally departs from the paper’s 40 ms / 3.4 ms preprocessing and 200 ms / 10 ms choice-decoder windows only because the user’s decoder task explicitly requires 50 ms bins.\n5. **Tone-onset input**: Use the earliest sample-start event in each trial as tone onset. This preserves replay-induced timing shifts visible in early-lick trials and is the most faithful raw-data representation of “time from tone onset”.\n6. **Photostim input**: Represent photostimulation as a time-varying binary series, not a static trial label, because the decoder task requests the on/off state at every time point.\n7. **Choice output edge case**: For hit/miss trials, decode actual lick direction from lick-event timing. For ignore trials with no lick, use the fallback hierarchy documented above. This is the only unavoidable ambiguity created by the target spec relative to the raw data.\n8. **Trial retention and alignment validity**: Retain early-lick, miss, hit, ignore, and photostimulation trials because these are either decoder outputs or decoder inputs. Do not apply the reference code’s `regular_trial_mask` wholesale. However, do require that the full neural decoding window is actually supported by the raw session observation interval.\n9. **NWB trial mapping rule**: Do not assume `units/is_good_trials.shape[1]` means “use the first N behavioral trials”. In several sessions the ephys-backed trials form an offset block relative to the full behavioral trial table; use raw go times plus `obs_intervals` to select valid trials.\n10. **Tongue discretization**: Discretize after session-level alignment and binning, using percentiles over the kept binned tongue-y values from that session, exactly as requested by the decoder task.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] For random good units/trials, compare converted spike counts in selected bins against direct counts from raw `spike_times` using bin-edge search.\n- [ ] For random trials, list of sessions\n    64→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    65→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    66→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    67→            ...\n    68→        ],\n    69→        [  # They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   126→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   127→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   128→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   129→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   130→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   131→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   132→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   133→\n   134→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   135→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   136→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   137→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   138→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   139→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   140→\n   141→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   142→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   143→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   144→\n   145→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   146→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   147→\n   148→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   149→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   150→\n   151→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   152→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   153→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   154→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   155→\n   156→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   157→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   158→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   159→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   160→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   161→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   162→\n   163→## Python environment\n   164→\n   165→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   166→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   167→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   168→\n   169→## Decoder Reference\n   170→\n   171→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   172→- Optional arguments:\n   173→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   174→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   175→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   176→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   177→\n   178→### Purpose\n   179→\n   180→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   181→1. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\n   223→4. **REMINDER**: Do NOT look at any files outside this directory\n   224→\n   225→**Done when**: \n   226→- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\n   227→- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\n   228→- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n   229→\n   230→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\n   231→\n   232→---\n   233→\n   234→### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n   235→\n   236→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   237→\n   238→**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n   239→\n   240→**Actions**:\n   241→- Look for documentation or README files in the `code` directory\n   242→- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference papers loaded and manipulated the data\n   243→- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n   244→- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n   245→- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n   246→- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n   247→\n   248→**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n   249→\n   250→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   251→\n   252→---\n   253→\n   254→### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n   255→\n   256→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   257→\n   258→**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\n   259→\n   260→**Actions**:\n   261→- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\n   262→- Determine available variables and their meanings\n   263→- Check data dimensions and data types\n   264→- Look for documentation or README files\n   265→- Note the data organization and structure\n   266→- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\n   267→\n   268→**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 2\".\n   269→\n   270→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   271→\n   272→---\n   273→\n   274→### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   275→\n   276→**Goal**: Extract key information from the papers and methods.\n   277→\n   278→**Actions**:\n   279→- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\n   280→- Look for and note to file:\n   281→  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\n   282→  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\n   283→  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\n   284→  - Variables important to experiments\n   285→  - Temporal alignment\n   286→  - Temporal binning\n   287→- Read the reference papers to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n   288→\n   289→**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n   290→\n   291→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   292→\n   293→---\n   294→\n   295→### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   296→\n   297→**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n   298→\n   299→**Actions**:\n   300→- Compare your understanding of:\n   301→  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n   302→  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n   303→  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n   304→- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n   305→- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n   306→  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n   307→  - Data processing\n   308→  - Data curation\n   309→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   310→\n   311→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   312→\n   313→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   314→\n   315→---\n   316→\n   317→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   318→\n   319→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   320→\n   321→**Actions**:\n   322→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   323→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   324→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   325→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   326→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   327→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   328→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   329→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   330→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   331→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   332→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   333→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   334→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   335→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   336→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   337→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   338→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   339→\n   340→**Done when**:\n   341→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   342→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   343→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   344→\n   345→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   346→\n   347→---\n   348→\n   349→### Step 6: Script Development\n   350→\n   351→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   352→\n   353→**Actions**:\n   354→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   355→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   356→- Include options:\n   357→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   358→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   359→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   360→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   361→- Write efficient code:\n   362→  - Vectorize loops\n   363→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   364→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   365→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   366→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   367→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   368→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   369→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   370→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   371→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   372→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   373→\n   374→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   375→\n   376→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   377→\n   378→---\n   379→\n   380→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   381→\n   382→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   383→\n   384→**Actions**:\n   385→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   386→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   387→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   388→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   389→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   390→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   391→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   392→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   393→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   394→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   395→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   396→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   397→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   398→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   399→\n   400→**Done when**: \n   401→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   402→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   403→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   404→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   405→\n   406→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   407→\n   408→---\n   409→\n   410→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   411→\n   412→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   413→\n   414→**Actions**:\n   415→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   416→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   417→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   418→\n   419→**Investigate any issues**:\n   420→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   421→\n   422→**Done when**: \n   423→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   424→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   425→- Accuracies are above chance\n   426→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   427→\n   428→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   429→\n   430→---\n   431→\n   432→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   433→\n   434→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   435→\n   436→**Actions**:\n   437→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   438→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   439→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   440→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   441→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   442→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   443→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   444→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   445→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   446→\n   447→**Done when**:\n   448→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   449→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   450→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   451→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   452→\n   453→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   454→\n   455→---\n   456→\n   457→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   458→\n   459→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   460→\n   461→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   462→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   463→\n   464→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   465→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   466→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   467→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   468→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   469→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   470→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   471→\n   472→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   473→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   474→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   475→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   476→- Fix any mismatches. \n   477→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   478→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   479→\n   480→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   481→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n   482→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   483→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   484→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   485→\n   486→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   487→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   488→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   489→\n   490→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   491→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   492→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   493→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   494→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   495→\n   496→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   497→\n   498→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   499→\n   500→---\n   501→\n   502→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   503→\n   504→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   505→\n   506→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   507→\n   508→**Actions**:\n   509→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   510→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   511→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   512→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n   513→\n   514→**Done when**: \n   515→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   516→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   517→\n   518→---\n   519→\n   520→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   521→\n   522→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   523→\n   524→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   525→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   526→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   527→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   528→\n   529→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n   530→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n   531→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\n   532→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   533→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   534→\n   535→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   536→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   537→\n   538→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   539→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   540→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   541→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   542→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   543→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n   544→\n   545→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   546→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   547→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   548→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   549→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   550→\n   551→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   552→\n   553→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   554→\n   555→---\n   556→\n   557→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   558→\n   559→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   560→\n   561→**Actions**:\n   562→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   563→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   564→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   565→  - Validation results and tables\n   566→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   567→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   568→  - Dataset description\n   569→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   570→  - Output format specification\n   571→  - Key statistics\n   572→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   573→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   574→\n   575→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   576→\n   577→---\n   578→\n   579→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   580→\n   581→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference papers and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- **Papers**:\n  - Data were first presented in \"Brain-wide neural activity underlying memory-guided movement\". It is in the file `datapaper.pdf`\n  - A recent paper that uses this dataset is \"Brain-wide analysis reveals movement encoding structured across and within brain areas\". It is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`\n  - A white paper about spike sorting and quality control is in the file `ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code**:\n  - `ecephys_spike_sorting` contains code used for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control.\n  - `code` contains code from `methodpaper.pdf`.\n- **Data** from the papers are in the directory `data`.\n- **Documentation** on the NWB file format is at https://nwb-schema.readthedocs.io. \n\n## Decoder Task\n\n- Temporally align based on **Go cue onset**. \n- Extract **2.5 s before to 1.5 s after** the go cue for each trial\n- Use **50-ms-width bins** for computing firing rates. \n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time from **tone onset** in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n- Whether **photostimulation** is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying)\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Lick direction **choice** (left = 0, right = 1, per-trial)\n- **Outcome** (ignore = 0, miss = 1, hit = 2, per-trial)\n- **Early lick** (no = 0, yes = 1, per-trial)\n- **Tongue y-position**, per-session discretization:\n  - 0: < 40th percentile of y-position over the session\n  - 1: 40th to 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n  - 2: > 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ] # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference papers and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n\n---\n\n## Conversion Workflow\n\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n\n---\n\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n\n**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n\n**Done when**: \n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n\n---\n\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n\n---\n\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n  - Key decisions and rationale\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n  - Validation results and tables\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n  - Dataset description\n  - How to load and use the converted data\n  - Output format specification\n  - Key statistics\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n\n---\n\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. 228→\n   229→    fig.tight_layout()\n   230→    fig.savefig(output_path, dpi=150)\n   231→    plt.close(fig)\n   232→\n   233→\n   234→def process_session(file_path: Path, show_processing: bool = 303→\n   304→    n_trials = len(trial_start)\n   305→    n_units = len(good_unit_idx)\n   306→    n_bins = len(bin_centers_rel)\n   307→\n   308→    # Per-trial task timing\n   309→ 334→            inp[1] = ((bin_centers_rel >= stim_rel_on) & (bin_centers_rel < stim_rel_off)).astype(np.float32)\n   335→            photostim_trial_count += 1\n   336→ 363→        bin_edges_rel=bin_edges_rel,\n   364→    )\n   365→    valid_values = tongue_y_binned[np.isfinite(tongue_y_binned)]\n   366→    if len(valid_values) == 0:\n   367→        raise tongue_discrete[tongue_y_binned > tongue_p60] = 2\n   373→\n   374→    output_trials = []\n   375→    for trial in range(n_trials):\n   376→        out = np.empty((4, n_bins), dtype=np.int8)\n   377→        out[0] = choice_trials[trial]\n   378→        out[1] = outcome_trials[trial]\n   379→        out[2] = early_trials[trial]\n   380→        out[3] = tongue_discrete[trial]\n   381→ 0.0\n   402→        neural_session[:, unit_pos, :] = fr.astype(np.float16)\n   403→\n   404→    nonzero_trial_mask = np.any(neural_session > 0, axis=(1, 2))\n   405→    dropped_zero_trials = choice_trials = choice_trials[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   412→        outcome_trials = outcome_trials[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   413→        early_trials = early_trials[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   414→        input_trials = [trial for keep, trial in zip(nonzero_trial_mask, input_trials) if keep]\n   415→        output_trials = [trial for keep, trial in zip(nonzero_trial_mask, output_trials) if keep]\n   416→        trial_start = trial_start[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   417→        trial_stop = trial_stop[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   418→        go_times = go_times[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   419→        sample_slice_starts = sample_slice_starts[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   420→        sample_slice_ends = sample_slice_ends[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   421→        delay_slice_starts = 427→        outcome = outcome[nonzero_trial_mask]\n   428→        is_good_trials = is_good_trials[:, nonzero_trial_mask]\n   429→        n_trials = int(neural_session.shape[0])\n   430→\n   431→    neural_trials = [neural_session[trial] for trial in range(n_trials)]\n   432→\n   433→    # Plot payload\n   434→    plot_payload = None\n   435→    if show_processing:\n   436→ 500→        session_id=session_id,\n   501→        subject_id=subject_id,\n   502→        source_file=str(file_path),\n   503→        neural=neural_trials,\n   504→        inputs=input_trials,\n 512→def build_dataset(results: list[SessionResult], mode: str) -> dict:\n   513→    subjects = sorted({r.subject_id for r in results})\n   514→    subject_to_idx = {subject: idx for idx, subject in enumerate(subjects)}\n   515→\n   516→    brain_regions = sorted({region for r in results for region in r.brain_region_names.tolist()})\n   517→    region_to_idx = {region: idx for idx, region in enumerate(brain_regions)}\n   518→\n   519→    brain_region_idx = [\n   520→        np.array([region_to_idx[name] for name in r.brain_region_names], dtype=np.int64)\n   521→        for r in results\n   522→    ]\n   523→\n   524→    metadata = {\n   525→        \"task_description\": (\n   526→            \"Auditory delayed-response task. Neural activity is aligned to go cue onset and used to decode \"\n   527→            \"choice, outcome, early lick, and discretized tongue y-position, with time-from-tone and \"\n   528→            \"photostimulation as decoder inputs.\"\n   529→        ),\n   530→        \"time_bin_size\": 50.0,\n   531→        \"time_bin_size_s\": BIN_WIDTH_S,\n   532→        \"temporal_alignment_event\": \"Go cue onset\",\n   533→        \"off_start\": WINDOW_START_S,\n   534→        \"off_end\": WINDOW_END_S,\n   535→        \"bin_centers_s\": (np.arange(WINDOW_START_S + BIN_WIDTH_S / 2.0, WINDOW_END_S, BIN_WIDTH_S)).astype(np.float32),\n   536→        \"source_dataset\": \"Mesoscale Activity Map Dataset (DANDI 000363)\",\n   537→        \"source_format\": \"NWB\",\n   538→        \"session_ids\": [r.session_id for r in results],\n   539→        \"source_files\": [r.source_file for r in results],\n   540→        \"conversion_mode\": mode,\n   541→        \"neuron_qc_rule\": \"units/classification == good; sessions with zero good units excluded\",\n   542→        \"trial_selection_rule\": (\n   543→            \"Keep trials whose full [-2.5 s, +1.5 s] go-aligned neural window lies inside the union of good-unit \"\n   544→            \"obs_intervals; combine this with units/is_good_trials when its column count matches the selected trials.\"\n   545→        ),\n   546→        \"brain_region_label_rule\": \"Exact units/anno_name CCF annotations for kept units\",\n   547→        \"choice_fallback_rule\": (\n   548→            \"first post-go lick; else first lick anywhere in trial; else trial instruction for no-lick ignore trials\"\n   549→        ),\n   550→        \"tongue_alignment_rule\": \"last tongue_y frame in each 50 ms bin\",\n   551→        \"tongue_discretization_rule\": \"per-session 40th and 60th percentiles over aligned binned tongue_y\",\n   552→        \"sample_onset_rule\": \"earliest sample_start_times event within each trial window\",\n   553→        \"n_sessions\": len(results),\n   554→        \"n_subjects\": len(subjects),\n   555→    }\n   556→\n   557→    return {\n   558→        \"neural\": [r.neural for r in results],\n   559→        \"input\": [r.inputs for r in results],\n   560→        \"output\": [r.outputs for r in results],\n   561→        \"subjects\": subjects,\n   562→        \"subject_idx\": np.array([subject_to_idx[r.subject_id] for r in results], dtype=np.int64),\n   563→        \"brain_regions\": brain_regions,\n   564→        \"brain_region_idx\": brain_region_idx,\n   565→        \"input_names\": [\"time_from_tone_onset_s\", \"photostim_on\"],\n   566→        \"output_names\": [\"choice\", \"outcome\", \"early_lick\", \"tongue_y_bin\"],\n   567→        \"output_values\": [\n   568→            [\"left\", \"right\"],\n   569→            [\"ignore\", \"miss\", \"hit\"],\n   570→            [\"no\", \"yes\"],\n   571→            [\"lt_p40\", \"p40_to_p60\", \"gt_p60\"],\n   572→        ],\n   573→        \"metadata\": metadata,\n   574→    }\n   575→\n   576→\n   577→def main():\n   578→    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=\"Convert MAP NWB sessions into decoder-ready pickle format.\")\n   579→    parser.add_argument(\"outpicklefile\", type=str, help=\"Output pickle file path.\")\n   580→    mode = parser.add_mutually_exclusive_group()\n   581→    mode.add_argument(\"--full\", action=\"store_true\", help=\"Process all sessions (default).\")\n   582→    mode.add_argument(\"--sample\", action=\"store_true\", help=\"Process only 2 sessions for testing.\")\n   583→    parser.add_argument(\n   584→        \"--show-processing\",\n   585→        action=\"store_true\",\n   586→        help=\"Save processing visualizations for up to 2 sessions as processing_<session_id>.png.\",\n   587→    )\n   588→    args = parser.parse_args()\n   589→\n   590→    run_mode = \"sample\" if args.sample else \"full\"\n   591→    data_dir = Path(\"/app/data\")\n   592→    out_path = Path(args.outpicklefile)\n   593→\n   594→    all_files = get_session_files(data_dir)\n   595→    if run_mode == \"sample\":\n   596→        target_files = all_files[:12]\n   597→    else:\n   598→        target_files = all_files\n   599→\n   600→    print(f\"Found {len(all_files)} NWB files\")\n   601→    print(f\"Mode: {run_mode}\")\n   602→    print(f\"Output: {out_path}\")\n   603→\n   604→    start_total = time.time()\n   605→    results: list[SessionResult] = []\n   606→    plotted = 0\n   607→\n   608→    for idx, file_path in enumerate(target_files, start=1):\n   609→        start_session = time.time()\n   610→        result = process_session(file_path, show_processing=args.show_processing and plotted < 2)\n   611→ cross-validation for logistic regression with PCA and scaling\n    to decode binary labels from neural population activity.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    X : np.ndarray\n        Input data of shape (n_time, n_trials, n_features).\n    y : np.ndarray\n        Labels of shape (n_trials,).\n    outer_k_folds : int, optional\n        Number of outer cross-validation folds, by default 5.\n
