The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\n     4→quency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\n     5→response trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n     6→\n     7→Two CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n     8→\n     9→Multi-regional electrophysiological recordings\n    10→\n    11→We designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n    12→\n    13→Extracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\n    14→\n    15→Probes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\n    16→\n    17→Photoinhibition\n    18→\n    19→Light from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\n    20→\n    21→To analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\n    22→time window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\n    23→\n    24→Spike sorting and quality control\n    25→\n    26→We developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\n    27→\n    28→The details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \n    29→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ca63578d-999b-47a7-955c-44878b03adbe","uuid":"c6ff47d9-f89e-425b-8419-cdcce8fbf13c","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/app/methods.txt","content":"Behavior and video tracking\n\nMice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\nquency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\nresponse trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\n\nWe designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\n\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\n\nSpike sorting and quality control\n\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\n\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). 247→\n   248→def temporal_alignment_embed_and_ephys(ephys_tt,embed_tt, embed_vecs, fr, dt = 0.0034):\n   249→    '''Align the embeding vectors and the firing rates in time.\n   250→    \n   251→    This assumes that the dt is the same for both datasets.\n   252→\n   253→    Parameters\n   254→    ----------\n   255→    ephys_tt : np.ndarray\n   256→        The time stamps for the ephys data.\n    The average R2 scores for the response period (old method).\n   349→\n   350→    '''\n   351→    dt = 0.0034\n   352→    tt = r2_dict['tt']\n   353→    r2_scores = r2_dict['r2_scores']\n   354→    y_test = r2_dict['y_test']\n   355→    y_pred = r2_dict['y_pred']\n   356→\n   357→    sample_mask = (tt >= sample_period[0]) * (tt < sample_period[1])\n   358→    sample_mask *= (tt >= (sample_period[0]-timeshift*dt)) * (tt < (sample_period[1]-timeshift*dt))\n   359→    delay_mask = (tt >= delay_period[0]) * (tt < delay_period[1])\n   360→    delay_mask *= (tt >= [neuron]\n   482→    '''\n   483→    trial_avg = fr.mean(axis = 1)\n   484→    mean_corr = np.zeros(fr.shape[-1])\n   485→    for i in range(fr.shape[-1]):\n   486→        mean_corr[i] = np.mean([np.corrcoef(trial_avg[:,i], fr[:,j,i])[0][1] for j in range(fr.shape[1])])\n   487→    \n   488→    trial_to_trial_var = np.mean(np.var(fr, axis = 1), axis = 0)\n   489→    return mean_corr, trial_to_trial_var\n   490→\n   491→\n   492→def calculate_selectivity(fr_R,fr_L, normalize = True):\n   493→    '''\n   494→    Returns the selectivity of each neuron.\n   495→\n   496→    Parameters\n   497→    ----------\n   498→    fr_R : np.ndarray\n   499→        The firing rates for the right trials [trials,neurons].\n   500→    fr_L : np.ndarray\n   501→        The firing rates for the left trials [trials,neurons].\n   502→    normalize : bool, optional\n   503→        If True, normalizes the selectivity by the standard deviations,\n   504→        resulting in d'. Otherwise, returns the difference in means. \n   505→        The default is True.\n   506→\n   507→    Returns\n   508→    -------\n   509→    selectivity : np.ndarray\n   510→        The selectivity for each neuron.\n   511→    '''\n   512→    mean_R = fr_R.mean(axis = 0)\n   513→    mean_L = fr_L.mean(axis = 0)\n   514→    std_R = fr_R.std(axis = 0)\n   515→    std_L = fr_L.std(axis = 0)\n   516→    if normalize:\n   517→        selectivity = (mean_R - mean_L) / np.sqrt((std_R**2 + std_L**2) / 2)\n   518→    else:\n   519→        selectivity = mean_R - mean_L\n   520→    return selectivity\n   521→\n   522→def calculate_auc(fr_R, fr_L, cv=5):\n   523→    '''\n   524→    Returns the AUC of each neuron.\n   525→\n   526→    Parameters\n   527→    ----------\n   528→    fr_R : np.ndarray\n   529→        The firing rates for the right trials [trials,neurons].\n   530→    fr_L : np.ndarray\n   531→        The firing rates for the left trials [trials,neurons].\n   532→    cv : int, optional\n   533→        The number of folds for cross-validation. The default is 5.\n   534→\n   535→    Returns\n   536→    -------\n   537→    auc : np.ndarray\n   538→        The AUC for each neuron.\n   539→    '''\n   540→    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression\n   541→    from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, GridSearchCV\n   542→    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score\n   543→    import numpy as np\n   544→\n   545→    # Initialize AUC array\n   546→    auc = np.zeros(fr_R.shape[1])\n   547→\n   548→    # Define parameter grid for logistic regression\n   549→    param_grid = {'C': [0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100]}\n   550→\n   551→    for i in range(fr_R.shape[1]):\n   552→        # Prepare data for the i-th neuron\n   553→        y = np.concatenate([np.ones(fr_R.shape[0]), np.zeros(fr_L.shape[0])])\n   554→        X = np.concatenate([fr_R[:, i], fr_L[:, i]]).reshape(-1, 1)\n   555→\n   556→        # Outer CV loop\n   557→        skf = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=cv)\n   558→        fold_aucs = []\n   559→\n   560→        for train_index, test_index in skf.split(X, y):\n   561→            X_train, X_test = X[train_index], X[test_index]\n   562→            y_train, y_test = y[train_index], y[test_index]\n   563→\n   564→            # Inner CV for hyperparameter tuning\n   565→            grid_search = GridSearchCV(\n   566→                LogisticRegression(solver='liblinear'),\n   567→                param_grid,\n   568→                cv=cv,\n   569→                scoring='roc_auc'\n   570→            )\n   571→            grid_search.fit(X_train, y_train)\n   572→\n   573→            # The default is 5.\n    \n    Returns\n    -------\n    r2_scores_per_fold : np.ndarray\n        The R² scores for each fold.\n    y_test_per_fold : np.ndarray\n        The target data for each fold.\n    y_pred_per_fold : np.ndarray\n        The predicted data for each fold.\n    test_masks : np.ndarray\n        The trial masks for each fold.\n    '''\n\n    kf = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=cv)\n    r2_scores_per_fold = []\n    y_test_per_fold = []\n    y_pred_per_fold = []\n    test_masks = []\n\n    for train_index, test_index in kf.split(X, trial_mask):\n        X_train, X_test = X[train_index], X[test_index]\n        y_train, y_test = y[train_index], y[test_index]\n        mask_train, mask_test = trial_mask[train_index], trial_mask[test_index]\n        test_masks.append(mask_test)\n\n        model.fit(X_train, y_train)\n        y_pred = model.predict(X_test)\n        r2_scores = r2_score(y_test, y_pred, multioutput='raw_values')\n\n        r2_scores_per_fold.append(r2_scores)\n        y_test_per_fold.append(y_test)\n        y_pred_per_fold.append(y_pred)\n\n    return np.array(r2_scores_per_fold).T, np.concatenate(y_test_per_fold, axis = 0), np.concatenate(y_pred_per_fold, axis = 0), np.concatenate(test_masks, axis = 0)\n\nfind_closest = lambda x, x0: np.argmin(np.abs(x-x0))\n\ndef temporal_alignment_embed_and_ephys(ephys_tt,embed_tt, embed_vecs, fr, dt = 0.0034):\n    '''Align the embeding vectors and the firing rates in time.\n    \n    This assumes that the dt is the same for both datasets.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    ephys_tt : np.ndarray\n        The time stamps for the ephys data.\n    embed_tt : np.ndarray\n        The time stamps for the embedding vectors.\n    embed_vecs : np.ndarray [time, trial, embedding_dim]\n        The embedding vectors.\n    fr : np.ndarray [time, trial, neuron]\n        The firing rates.\n    dt : float, optional\n        The time step. The default is 0.0034.\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    joint_tt : np.ndarray\n        The time stamps for the joint data.\n    joint_embed_vecs : np.ndarray\n        The embedding vectors aligned to the ephys data.\n    joint_fr : np.ndarray\n        The firing rates aligned to the embedding vectors.\n    '''\n    if ephys_tt[0] < embed_tt[0]:\n        embed_start_ind = 0\n        ephys_start_ind = find_closest(ephys_tt, embed_tt[0] + dt/2)\n    else: \n        embed_start_ind = find_closest(embed_tt, ephys_tt[0] - dt/2)\n        ephys_start_ind = 0\n\n    if ephys_tt[-1] > embed_tt[-1] + dt/2:\n        embed_end_ind = len(embed_tt)\n        ephys_end_ind = ephys_start_ind + embed_end_ind - embed_start_ind\n    else:\n        ephys_end_ind = len(ephys_tt)\n        embed_end_ind = embed_start_ind + ephys_end_ind - ephys_start_ind\n\n    joint_tt = ephys_tt[ephys_start_ind:ephys_end_ind]\n    joint_embed_vecs = embed_vecs[embed_start_ind:embed_end_ind]\n    joint_fr = fr[ephys_start_ind:ephys_end_ind]\n\n    return joint_tt, joint_embed_vecs, joint_fr\n\ndef get_regular_trial_mask(ephys_data):\n    '''\n    Returns a mask for regular trials.\n    \n    No early lick, no auto water, no free water, no no response trials.\n    '''\n    return (ephys_data['early_lick_trials'] == 0) \\\n         * (ephys_data['auto_water_trials'] == 0)  \\\n         * (ephys_data['free_water_trials'] == 0)  \\\n         * (ephys_data['correctness'] != -1) \\\n         * (ephys_data['stimulation'][:,0] == 0)\n\n\ndef process_single_session_r2_dict(\n        r2_dict, \n        sample_period = [-1.85,-1.2], \n        delay_period = [-1.2,0.], \n        response_period = [0, 1.5],\n        timeshift = 0):\n    '''\n    Get average R2 scores for sample, delay and response periods.\n\n    Calculates both the averages of the instantaneois R2 scores.\n    And the R2 scores for all the trials and timepoints concatenated \n    within an epoch. This is the \"old method\". \n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    r2_dict : dict\n        The dictionary containing the R2 scores.\n    sample_period : list, optional\n        The sample period. The default is [-1.85,-1.2].\n    delay_period : list, optional\n        The delay period. The default is [-1.2,0.].\n    response_period : list, optional\n        The response period. The default is [0, 1.5].\n    timeshift : float, optional\n        If there was timeshift between the video and firing rates.\n        The default is 0.\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    sample_r2 : np.ndarray\n        The average R2 scores for the sample period.\n    delay_r2 : np.ndarray\n        The average R2 scores for the delay period.\n    response_r2 : np.ndarray   \n        The average R2 scores for the response period.\n    sample_r2_old : np.ndarray\n        The average R2 scores for the sample period (old method).\n    delay_r2_old : np.ndarray\n        The average R2 scores for the delay period (old method).\n    response_r2_old : np.ndarray\n        The average R2 scores for the response period (old method).\n\n    '''\n    dt = 0.0034\n    tt = r2_dict['tt']\n    r2_scores = r2_dict['r2_scores']\n    y_test = r2_dict['y_test']\n    y_pred = r2_dict['y_pred']\n\n    sample_mask = (tt >= sample_period[0]) * (tt < sample_period[1])\n    sample_mask *= (tt >= (sample_period[0]-timeshift*dt)) * (tt < (sample_period[1]-timeshift*dt))\n    delay_mask = (tt >= delay_period[0]) * (tt < delay_period[1])\n    delay_mask *= (tt >= (delay_period[0]-timeshift*dt)) * (tt < (delay_period[1]-timeshift*dt))\n    response_mask = (tt >= response_period[0]) * (tt < response_period[1])\n    response_mask *= (tt >= (response_period[0]-timeshift*dt)) * (tt < (response_period[1]-timeshift*dt))\n\n    sample_r2 = np.mean(r2_scores[sample_mask,:], axis = (0,2))\n    delay_r2 = np.mean(r2_scores[delay_mask,:], axis = (0,2))\n    response_r2 = np.mean(r2_scores[response_mask,:], axis = (0,2))\n\n    sample_r2_old = r2_score(\n        np.concatenate(y_test[sample_mask],axis = 0),\n        np.concatenate(y_pred[sample_mask],axis = 0),\n        multioutput='raw_values')\n    delay_r2_old = r2_score(\n        np.concatenate(y_test[delay_mask],axis = 0),\n        np.concatenate(y_pred[delay_mask],axis = 0),\n        multioutput='raw_values')\n    response_r2_old = r2_score(\n        np.concatenate(y_test[response_mask],axis = 0),\n        np.concatenate(y_pred[response_mask],axis = 0),\n        multioutput='raw_values')\n    \n    return sample_r2, delay_r2, response_r2, sample_r2_old, delay_r2_old, response_r2_old\n\ndef process_single_session_r2_dict_keepfolds(\n        r2_dict, \n        sample_period = [-1.7,-1.35], \n        delay_period = [-1.05,-0.15], \n        response_period = [0.15, 1.2],):\n    '''\n    Get average R2 scores for sample, delay and response periods.\n\n    Calculates both the averages of the instantaneois R2 scores.\n    And the R2 scores for all the trials and timepoints concatenated \n    within an epoch. response_period[0]) * (tt < response_period[1])\n\n    sample_r2 = np.mean(r2_scores[sample_mask,:], axis = (0))\n    delay_r2 = np.mean(r2_scores[delay_mask,:], axis = (0))\n    response_r2 = \n        delay_period = [-1.2,0.], \n        response_period = [0, 1.5]):\n    '''\n    Get the average firing rates for the sample, delay and response periods.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    fr : np.ndarray\n        The firing rates [time,trial,neuron].\n    tt : np.ndarray\n        The time stamps.\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    sample_fr : np.ndarray\n        The average firing rates for the sample period.\n    delay_fr : np.ndarray\n        The average firing rates for the delay period.\n    response_fr : np.ndarray\n        The average firing rates for the response period.\n    '''\n    \n    sample_mask = (tt >= sample_period[0]) * (tt < sample_period[1])\n    delay_mask = (tt >= delay_period[0]) * (tt < delay_period[1])\n    response_mask = (tt >= response_period[0]) * (tt < response_period[1])\n\n    sample_fr = np.mean(fr[sample_mask,:], axis = (0,1))\n    delay_fr = np.mean(fr[delay_mask,:], axis = (0,1))\n    response_fr = np.mean(fr[response_mask,:], axis = (0,1))\n\n    return sample_fr, delay_fr, response_fr\n\ndef get_mean_corr_and_trial_to_trial_variance(fr):\n    '''\n    Get the mean correlation and trial to trial variance for the firing rates.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    fr : np.ndarray\n        The firing rates [time,trial,neuron].\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    mean_corr : np.ndarray\n        Mean correlation with the trial averaged firing rate. [neuron]\n    trial_to_trial_var : np.ndarray\n        Time averaged trial to trial variance. [neuron]\n    '''\n    trial_avg = fr.mean(axis = 1)\n    mean_corr = np.zeros(fr.shape[-1])\n    for i in range(fr.shape[-1]):\n        mean_corr[i] = np.mean([np.corrcoef(trial_avg[:,i], fr[:,j,i])[0][1] for j in range(fr.shape[1])])\n    \n    trial_to_trial_var = np.mean(np.var(fr, axis = 1), axis = 0)\n    return mean_corr, trial_to_trial_var\n\n\ndef calculate_selectivity(fr_R,fr_L, normalize = True):\n    '''\n    Returns the selectivity of each neuron.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    fr_R : np.ndarray\n        The firing rates for the right trials [trials,neurons].\n    fr_L : np.ndarray\n        The firing rates for the left trials [trials,neurons].\n    normalize : bool, optional\n        If True, normalizes the selectivity by the standard deviations,\n        resulting in d'. Otherwise, returns the difference in means. \n        The default is True.\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    selectivity : np.ndarray\n        The selectivity for each neuron.\n    '''\n    mean_R = fr_R.mean(axis = 0)\n    mean_L = fr_L.mean(axis = 0)\n    std_R = fr_R.std(axis = 0)\n    std_L = fr_L.std(axis = 0)\n    if normalize:\n        selectivity = (mean_R - mean_L) / np.sqrt((std_R**2 + std_L**2) / 2)\n    else:\n        selectivity = mean_R - mean_L\n    return selectivity\n\ndef calculate_auc(fr_R, fr_L, cv=5):\n    '''\n    Returns the AUC of each neuron.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    fr_R : np.ndarray\n        The firing rates for the right trials [trials,neurons].\n    fr_L : np.ndarray\n        The firing rates for the left trials [trials,neurons].\n    cv : int, optional\n        The number of folds for cross-validation. The default is 5.\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    auc : np.ndarray\n        The AUC for each neuron.\n    '''\n    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression\n    from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, GridSearchCV\n    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score\n    import numpy as np\n\n    # Initialize AUC array\n    auc = np.zeros(fr_R.shape[1])\n\n    # Define parameter grid for logistic regression\n    param_grid = {'C': [0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100]}\n\n    for i in range(fr_R.shape[1]):\n        # Prepare data for the i-th neuron\n        y = np.concatenate([np.ones(fr_R.shape[0]), np.zeros(fr_L.shape[0])])\n        X = np.concatenate([fr_R[:, i], fr_L[:, i]]).reshape(-1, 1)\n\n        # Outer CV loop\n        skf = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=cv)\n        fold_aucs = []\n\n        for train_index, test_index in skf.split(X, y):\n            X_train, X_test = X[train_index], X[test_index]\n            y_train, y_test = y[train_index], y[test_index]\n\n            # Inner CV for hyperparameter tuning\n            grid_search = GridSearchCV(\n                LogisticRegression(solver='liblinear'),\n                param_grid,\n                cv=cv,\n                scoring='roc_auc'\n            )\n            grid_search.fit(X_train, y_train)\n\n            # Best logistic regression model\n            best_model = grid_search.best_estimator_\n\n            # Evaluate on the test set\n            y_pred = best_model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]\n            fold_aucs.append(roc_auc_score(y_test, y_pred))\n\n        # Average AUC across folds\n        auc[i] = np.mean(fold_aucs)\n\n    return auc\n","numLines":584,"startLine":1,"totalLines":584}}}

This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    45→    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n    46→        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    47→    else:\n    48→        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    49→    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n    50→\n    51→    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n    52→\n    53→    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    54→    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n    55→        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n    56→\n    57→    if rate:\n    58→        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    59→    return binCenters, binSpikes\n    60→\n    61→\n    62→def process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='classifier'):\n    63→    \"\"\"\n    64→    Process all probe files in one session.\n    65→\n    66→    Args:\n    67→        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\n    68→        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\n    69→        bw (float): in sec.\n    70→        stride (float): in sec.\n    71→        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\n    72→    \"\"\"\n    73→    print(qc_mode)\n    74→    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\n    75→        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\n    76→        print(qc_folder)\n    77→    elif qc_mode == 'old':\n    78→        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\n    79→    else:\n    80→        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode! Empty 0x1 cell. Need to manually define what it becomes in Python.\n2. 1x1 cell. Will be directly read as non-iterables such as str, int, float, etc. Need to manually convert it to an iterable with length 1.\n3. Nx1 cell (N >= 2). Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\n\nComment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\n    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\n        do something\n\"\"\"\n\nplt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\nplt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\n\n\ndef sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\n    '''\n    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\n    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\n    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\n    '''\n    bin_begin_time = begin_time\n    bin_end_time = end_time\n    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    else:\n        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n\n    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n\n    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n\n    if rate:\n        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    return binCenters, binSpikes\n\n\ndef process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='classifier'):\n    \"\"\"\n    Process all probe files in one session.\n\n    Args:\n        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\n        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\n        bw (float): in sec.\n        stride (float): in sec.\n        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\n    \"\"\"\n    print(qc_mode)\n    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\n        print(qc_folder)\n    elif qc_mode == 'old':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\n    else:\n        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode! Skip.' % sess_name)\n            return # HERE\n\n        print('Reading probe file', probe)\n        # sess_file = utils.loadmat(os.path.join(file_path))\n        sess_file = preprocessing_utils.loadmat(file_path)\n\n        # --- 1.1 behaviors and tasks ---\n        auto_learn_trials = sess_file['behavior_auto_learn'] # ndarray of str, '1' is autolearn, '4' is non-autolearn.\n        auto_learn_trials = np.asarray(auto_learn_trials, dtype=int)\n        early_lick_trials = sess_file['behavior_early_report'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n        auto_water_trials = sess_file['behavior_is_auto_water'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n        free_water_trials = sess_file['behavior_is_free_water'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n        lick_directions = sess_file['behavior_lick_directions'] # ndarray, (n_trials). First row is go cue time, second row is duration of response period\n        delay_time = sess_file['task_delay_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is delay time, second row is duration of delay period\n        sample_time = sess_file['task_sample_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is sample time, second row is duration of sample period\n        delay_period = delay_time[1,:] # only need duration of delay period\n        sample_period = sample_time[1,:] # only need duration of sample period\n        stimulation = sess_file['task_stimulation'] # ndarray, (n_trials,4). Each element is a list of 0 (lick right) and 1 (lick left)\n            sess_dict['lick_times'] = lick_time_cal # list with length n_trials, each element is 1D ndarray with all lick times in that trial\n            sess_dict['gocue_time'] = gocue_time[0,:]\n            sess_dict['correctness'] = correctness # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64. 1: correct or free water, 0: error, -1: no response\n            sess_dict['delay_period'] = delay_period # (n_trials,) duration of delay period\n            sess_dict['sample_period'] = sample_period # (n_trials,) duration of sample period\n            sess_dict['stimulation'] = stimulation # ndarray, (n_trials,4). The 4 columns are [laser_power, stim_type('1', '2', or '6', with 1,2,6 being left/right/both ALM perturbation), laser_on_time, laser_off_time]. For no stim trials, we have [0, NaN, NaN, NaN]\n            sess_dict['trial_type'] = 1*(trial_type=='l') # ndarray, (n_trials,). 1 is left, 0 is right 'r'\n            # with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'behavior_task.pickle'), 'wb') as f:\n            #     pickle.dump(sess_dict, f)\n\n            ### Read the neuron IDs after QC for this session ###\n            '''\n            The index refers to within individual SESSION, resulting in summing all units from all penetrations in a given Session. For instance, if in Session 08/17/22, we have 3 penetrations: p1 has 10 units, p2 has 30 units, p3 has 5 units (before filtering) - overall there are 45 units. An index of '42' from this session means the 2nd (42-10-30) unit from p3.\n            '''\n            temp_list = list(glob.glob(os.path.join(qc_folder, '{}*.mat'.format(sess_name_qc))))\n            assert len(temp_list) == 1\n            filepath_qc = temp_list[0]\n\n            qc_file = preprocessing_utils.loadmat(filepath_qc)\n            # idx_qc_dict = qc_file['Idx'] # dict. Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n            idx_qc_dict = {}# dict. Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n            for area, id_list in qc_file['Idx'].items():\n                if id_list == []:\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = []\n                elif isinstance(id_list, int) or isinstance(id_list, float): # only one neuron\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = [id_list-1]\n                else:\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = list(np.array(id_list) - 1)\n\n            anno_qc_dict = {}\n            for name, value in qc_file['AnnoName'].items():\n                if isinstance(value, str):\n                    anno_qc_dict[name] = [value]\n                else:\n                    anno_qc_dict[name] = value\n\n        # --- 1.2 neural data ---\n        # -- 1.2.1 spike times\n        spike_times = sess_file['neuron_single_units'] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n\n        # -- 1.2.2 unit_info and QC stats\n        unit_info = sess_file['neuron_unit_info'] # ndarray (n_units, 8)\n        # neuron_unit_info: columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location. For putative type, we have: 'Pyr' - pyramidal neurons, 'FS' - fast spiking, 'not classified', 'all' - all types\n        probe_no_col = probe * np.ones((unit_info.shape[0], 1)) # (n_units,1) original probe #\n        unit_info = np.concatenate([unit_info, probe_no_col], axis=1) # (n_units, 9)\n        unit_qc = sess_file['neuron_unit_quality_control']\n        # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n\n        # -- 1.2.3 histology\n        histology = sess_file['histology'] # dictionary, keys: 'unit', 'ccf_x', 'ccf_y', 'ccf_z', 'annotation'\n        ccf_unit_id = np.array(histology['unit']) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n        ccf_coordinate = np.stack([histology['ccf_x'], histology['ccf_y'], histology['ccf_z']]).swapaxes(0,1) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n        ccf_label = histology['annotation'] # list of np.str_\n        ccf_label = [str(x).strip() for x in ccf_label] # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n\n        # -- 1.2.4 get units that have both ephys and histology\n        unit_comb = set(unit_info[:,0]) & set(ccf_unit_id) # get the id of units that have both ephys and histology\n        mask_ephys = [True if x in unit_comb else False for x in unit_info[:,0]] # mask for all other data (ephys) except histology\n        mask_histology =[True if x in unit_comb else False for x in ccf_unit_id] # mask for histology only\n\n        # mask CCF info\n        ccf_coordinate = ccf_coordinate[mask_histology,:] # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n        ccf_label = list(compress(ccf_label, mask_histology)) # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n        ccf_unit_id = ccf_unit_id[mask_histology]\n\n        # mask ephys info\n        unit_info = unit_info[mask_ephys,:] # (n_neurons, 9)\n        spike_times = spike_times[mask_ephys]\n        for name in unit_qc.keys():\n            unit_qc[name] = list(np.array(unit_qc[name])[mask_ephys])\n\n        # --- 1.3 add probe data to sess data ---\n        spike_times_sess.append(spike_times) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n        unit_info_sess.append(unit_info) # (n_units, 9)\n        unit_qc_sess_list.append(unit_qc) # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n        ccf_coordinate_sess.append(ccf_coordinate) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n        ccf_label_sess = ccf_label_sess + ccf_label # list\n        ccf_unit_id_sess.append(ccf_unit_id) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n\n    # ----- 2. combine data from all probes ---\n    spike_times_sess = np.concatenate(spike_times_sess) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n    unit_info_sess = np.concatenate(unit_info_sess, axis=0) # (n_units, 9)\n\n    # uncommented\n    unit_qc_sess = {'unit_amp': [], 'presence_ratio': [], 'amplitude_cutoff': [], 'isi_violation': [], 'avg_firing_rate':[], 'drift_metric': []}\n    for probe_qc_dict in unit_qc_sess_list:\n        for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n            unit_qc_sess[qc_name] = unit_qc_sess[qc_name] + probe_qc_dict[qc_name]\n\n    ccf_coordinate_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_coordinate_sess, axis=0) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n    ccf_unit_id_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_unit_id_sess) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n\n    n_neurons = len(spike_times_sess)\n    assert unit_info_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n    assert len(unit_qc_sess['drift_metric']) == n_neurons\n    assert ccf_coordinate_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n    assert len(ccf_label_sess) == n_neurons\n    assert len(ccf_unit_id_sess) == n_neurons\n    # print('total neurons:', n_neurons)\n\n    # ----- 3. Process neurons from areas of interest and perform QC filtering -----\n    i_file = 1\n    neuron_id_used = []\n    neuron_id_all = np.arange(n_neurons) # neuron_id is continuous from 0. unit_id is the original id from DJ.\n\n    for region in ['ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'Pons', 'Cerebellum', 'Hypothalamus', 'Hippocampus', 'Orbital', 'OtherCortex', 'Olfactory', 'CorticalSubplate', 'Pallidum']:\n        for side in ['left', 'right']:\n            area = '_'.join([side, region])\n            area_key = '{}_qc'.format(region)\n            neuron_id_area = helper_get_neuron_id_area(idx_qc_dict[area_key], anno_qc_dict[area_key], ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, side, region)\n\n            if len(neuron_id_area) == 0:\n                continue\n\n            print('Filtered neurons:', area, len(neuron_id_area))\n\n            # record used neurons\n            neuron_id_used = neuron_id_used + neuron_id_area\n\n            save_flag = process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n\n            if save_flag:\n                i_file += 1\n\n    # ----- 4. Don't process the rest of the neurons -----\n    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'others_finished.txt'), 'w') as f:\n        f.write('{}: finished.'.format(sess_name))\n\n    '''\n    neuron_id_others = set(neuron_id_all) - set(neuron_id_used)\n    neuron_id_others = sorted(list(neuron_id_others))\n\n    if len(neuron_id_others)==0:\n        return\n\n    print('Filtered neurons:', 'others', len(neuron_id_others))\n    process_one_area(sess_name, 'others', neuron_id_others, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n    '''\n\n\ndef process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict):\n    \"\"\"\n    Get all neurons in an area, extract firing rates, and then save data.\n    \"\"\"\n    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n        return True\n\n    # uncommented\n    unit_qc_area = {}\n    for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n        unit_qc_area[qc_name] = np.array(unit_qc_sess[qc_name])[neuron_id_area]\n        assert len(unit_qc_sess[qc_name]) == n_units\n\n    ccf_coordinate_area = ccf_coordinate_sess[neuron_id_area,:] # HERE\n            area_dict_exist = pickle.load(f)\n            fr = area_dict_exist['fr']\n            spike_times_area = area_dict_exist['spike_times']\n            bin_centers = area_dict_exist['bin_centers']\n\n    # (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n    area_dict['spike_times'] = spike_times_area # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray with all spike times of this neuron in this trial. (go cue time is 0.)\n    area_dict['neuron_info'] = unit_info_area # ndarray (n_units, 9)\n    #  columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location, probe #. filter neurons using side\n    ML_mid_coor = 5700\n    if side == 'left':\n        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] >= ML_mid_coor\n    elif side == 'right':\n        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] < ML_mid_coor\n    neuron_id_side = neurons_all[mask_side]\n\n    if len(neuron_id_side) == 0:\n        return []\n\n    # temp = list(np.array(ccf_label)[neuron_id_area])\n    # print(len(temp), len(anno_qc_list))\n    # num = np.amin([len(temp), len(anno_qc_list)])\n    # for ii in range(num):\n        # if temp[ii] != anno_qc_list[ii]:\n        # (go cue time is 0.)\n    data_area['auto_learn_trials'] = data_sess['auto_learn_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n    data_area['early_lick_trials'] = data_sess['early_lick_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n    data_area['auto_water_trials'] = data_sess['auto_water_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n    data_area['free_water_trials'] = data_sess['free_water_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n    data_area['lick_directions'] = data_sess['lick_directions'] # ndarray, (n_trials). For no stim trials, we have [0, NaN, NaN, NaN]\n    data_area['trial_type'] = data_sess['trial_type'] # ndarray, (n_trials,). 1 is left, 0 is right 'r'\n\n    return data_area\n\n\ndef process_all_sess(bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, data_folder, save_folder, qc_mode):\n    # scan through all sessions, and find files within the same session\n    # all_path_list = sorted(list(glob.glob(os.path.join(data_folder, 'MAP_dandi', 'map*.mat')))) # SC065_20210507_172825_s8\n    all_path_list = sorted(list(glob.glob(os.path.join(data_folder, 'MAP_dandi', 'map-export_SC050_20210302_140243_s20_p*.mat')))) # SC011_20190222_130111_s4\n\n    name2paths = OrderedDict() # keys are part of file names that are the same for all sessions. The NWB `obs_intervals` field might be more restrictive.\n\nActually, looking more carefully: `good_unit_obs_intervals` has shape `(n_good_units, 2)` (not `(n_good_units, n_intervals, 2)`). The AI takes `np.min([:, 0])` and `np.max([:, 1])`, which is the earliest start and latest end of any good unit's observation interval. This is actually quite permissive - it's the union of all observation intervals. So trials that fall within ANY good unit's observation interval are kept.\n\nBut the result is only 51,346 trials out of ~95,000. That's a big reduction. The NWB `obs_intervals` for each unit describes when that unit was actually being observed/recorded. If the session recording window doesn't start until after the first few trials, those early trials wouldn't have valid neural data. Similarly for trials after recording ends. So this filter ensures neural data actually exists for the trial window, which is reasonable. The reference code doesn't do this explicitly because in their .mat format, the spike times are already pre-aligned to the go cue and presumably only include trials where data was recorded. So the two approaches may end up with similar trial sets, just through different mechanisms.\n\nThe NWB `obs_intervals` is a per-unit field, and it's possible that the obs_intervals in NWB are more restrictive than what the .mat files contain. I shouldn't overthink this and should focus on documenting and evaluating.\n\nRegarding `is_good_trials` usage: the AI tries to use `units/is_good_trials` when its column count matches selected trials. This is an NWB-specific quality flag. The reference code doesn't have an equivalent because they use .mat files.\n\nThe AI also drops trials where ALL neurons have zero firing rate (`nonzero_trial_mask`). This is an additional filter not in the reference code.\n\nFor question 6-c about \"Distance to reward zone\" - this seems like a mistake in the template, as there is no \"distance to reward zone\" output. The instructions have \"Outcome\" as the output. I should address this as it relates to outcome alignment.\n\nNow I'm writing the two files: DECISIONS.md and llm_judge_eval.json. For the efficiency questions 10-a through 10-d, I need to analyze the code's performance characteristics. The spike binning loop and h5 file reading are the most time-consuming operations. The per-trial loops for input construction, choice inference, and sample_onset could be vectorized more effectively. There's also redundant computation where `event_slices_for_trials` is called twice and `abs_edges` is computed multiple times.\n\nAdditionally, some loaded data like tongue_likelihood and delay_start_times aren't being used in the outputs, and there's potential inefficiency where invalid neural data is set to zero but those same trials might be filtered out later How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The AI discovers all NWB files by globbing `data/sub-*/*.nwb`, yielding one HDF5 file per session. Each file is opened with `h5py` (not PyNWB) and the relevant groups (`units`, `intervals/trials`, `acquisition/BehavioralEvents`, `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries`) are read into numpy arrays. The AI processes sessions sequentially in a for-loop, constructing a `SessionResult` per session, then assembles them into the final dictionary.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef get_session_files(data_dir: Path) -> list[Path]:\n    return sorted(data_dir.glob(\"sub-*/*.nwb\"))\n\n# In process_session():\nwith h5py.File(file_path, \"r\") as h5:\n    subject_id, session_id = get_session_identity(file_path)\n    units = h5[\"units\"]\n    trials = h5[\"intervals\"][\"trials\"]\n    # ... reads trial_start_all, trial_stop_all, go_times_all, etc.\n```\n\niii. The AI chose `h5py` over PyNWB for speed, noting that \"Full PyNWB object loading produces avoidable overhead and warning spam.\" This is consistent with the data being in NWB/HDF5 format. The reference code loads `.mat` files exported from DataJoint, but the underlying data is the same MAP dataset.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Subjects are identified from the parent directory name of each NWB file (e.g., `sub-440956`). The subject ID is extracted by stripping the `sub-` prefix. Unique subjects are collected and sorted alphabetically to form the `subjects` list, with `subject_idx` mapping each session to its subject.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef get_session_identity(file_path: Path) -> tuple[str, str]:\n    subject_id = file_path.parent.name.replace(\"sub-\", \"\")\n    session_id = file_path.stem\n    return subject_id, session_id\n\n# In build_dataset():\nsubjects = sorted({r.subject_id for r in results})\nsubject_to_idx = {subject: idx for idx, subject in enumerate(subjects)}\n```\n\niii. This is the natural way to extract subject identity from the DANDI-structured NWB dataset. The resulting 28 subjects match the paper's reported count.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each NWB file corresponds to one session. The session ID is the file stem (e.g., `sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_behavior+ecephys+ogen`). Sessions with zero `classification == good` units are skipped, yielding 173 sessions matching the paper.\n\nii.\n```python\n# In process_session():\nclassification = np.char.lower(decode_str_array(units[\"classification\"][:]))\ngood_unit_idx = np.flatnonzero(classification == \"good\")\nif len(good_unit_idx) == 0:\n    print(f\"Skipping {session_id}: zero good units\")\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that 174 raw NWB files exist, but one session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508`) has zero good units, so after excluding it, 173 sessions remain, consistent with the paper's \"69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions.\"\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are read from the `intervals/trials` table in each NWB file. The AI extracts all trial start/stop times, behavioral labels (instruction, early_lick, outcome, photostim), and go cue times. A subset of these trials is then selected based on whether the full neural extraction window [-2.5s, +1.5s] relative to the go cue falls within the session's observation interval.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_start_all = trials[\"start_time\"][:].astype(np.float64)\ntrial_stop_all = trials[\"stop_time\"][:].astype(np.float64)\ngo_times_all = h5[\"acquisition\"][\"BehavioralEvents\"][\"go_start_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:n_behavior_trials].astype(np.float64)\n\nselected_trial_idx, session_obs_start, session_obs_stop = select_trial_indices(\n    go_times_all=go_times_all,\n    good_unit_obs_intervals=unit_obs_intervals,\n)\n```\n\niii. The AI justifies this by noting that some trials occur outside the neural recording window, so including them would yield meaningless neural data. The CONVERSION_NOTES document that many sessions have offset or shortened valid-trial blocks.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI applies two trial filters: (1) a session-level observation-interval filter that retains only trials whose full [-2.5, +1.5]s go-aligned neural window lies within the union of good-unit observation intervals, and (2) a post-hoc filter that drops trials where ALL neurons have zero firing rate across all time bins. The AI does NOT apply the reference code's `get_regular_trial_mask` (which excludes early-lick, auto-water, free-water, no-response, and photostimulation trials). Additionally, per-unit `is_good_trials` masks are used to zero out neural activity for specific neuron-trial combinations.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef select_trial_indices(go_times_all, good_unit_obs_intervals):\n    session_obs_start = float(np.min(good_unit_obs_intervals[:, 0]))\n    session_obs_stop = float(np.max(good_unit_obs_intervals[:, 1]))\n    full_window_mask = (\n        (go_times_all + WINDOW_START_S >= session_obs_start)\n        & (go_times_all + WINDOW_END_S <= session_obs_stop)\n    )\n    trial_idx = np.flatnonzero(full_window_mask)\n    return trial_idx, session_obs_start, session_obs_stop\n\n# Post-hoc zero-trial filter:\nnonzero_trial_mask = np.any(neural_session > 0, axis=(1, 2))\nif dropped_zero_trials:\n    neural_session = neural_session[nonzero_trial_mask]\n    # ... also filters all input/output arrays\n```\n\niii. The AI reasoned that early lick, outcome, and photostimulation should be retained since they are decoder inputs/outputs. The zero-firing-rate filter was added as a robustness measure. The result is 51,346 total trials across 173 sessions (mean 296.8/session), significantly fewer than the raw ~95,000 behavioral trials.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times` (ragged array of spike timestamps) for units where `units/classification == \"good\"`. The `spike_times_index` array is used to index into the ragged spike_times array for each unit.\n\nii.\n```python\nspike_times = units[\"spike_times\"][:].astype(np.float64)\nspike_times_index = units[\"spike_times_index\"][:]\nspike_starts, spike_ends = get_ragged_row_bounds(spike_times_index)\n\n# For each unit:\nspikes = spike_times[spike_starts[unit_idx]:spike_ends[unit_idx]]\n```\n\niii. This directly uses the raw spike times from Kilosort2 spike sorting, filtered by the classifier-based QC that labels units as \"good.\" This is consistent with the reference approach.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Raw spike times are binned into non-overlapping 50ms bins aligned to the go cue, covering [-2.5s, +1.5s]. Spike counts are converted to firing rates (Hz) by dividing by the bin width (0.05s). The result is stored as float16.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH_S = 0.05\nWINDOW_START_S = -2.5\nWINDOW_END_S = 1.5\n\nabs_edges = go_times[:, None] + bin_edges_rel[None, :]\nflat_edges = abs_edges.reshape(-1)\nfor unit_pos, unit_idx in enumerate(good_unit_idx):\n    spikes = spike_times[spike_starts[unit_idx]:spike_ends[unit_idx]]\n    edge_indices = np.searchsorted(spikes, flat_edges, side=\"left\").reshape(n_trials, n_bins + 1)\n    counts = np.diff(edge_indices, axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n    fr = counts / bin_width\n    neural_session[:, unit_pos, :] = fr.astype(np.float16)\n```\n\niii. The AI chose 50ms non-overlapping bins as explicitly required by the task instructions (\"Use 50-ms-width bins for computing firing rates\"). The reference code uses `bw=0.04, stride=0.0034` (40ms Gaussian-like bins with 3.4ms stride), but the task instructions override this.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `units/classification == \"good\"` are included. When `is_good_trials` column count matches the selected trials, per-unit-trial validity masks are used to zero out firing rates for invalid neuron-trial combinations. When it doesn't match, per-unit observation intervals are used to determine valid trials. Finally, trials with all-zero neural activity are dropped entirely.\n\nii.\n```python\nclassification = np.char.lower(decode_str_array(units[\"classification\"][:]))\ngood_unit_idx = np.flatnonzero(classification == \"good\")\n\n# Per-unit validity:\nif uses_direct_is_good_trials:\n    invalid_trials = ~is_good_trials[unit_pos]\nelse:\n    obs_start = unit_obs_intervals[unit_pos, 0]\n    obs_stop = unit_obs_intervals[unit_pos, 1]\n    valid_obs = (go_times + WINDOW_START_S >= obs_start) & (go_times + WINDOW_END_S <= obs_stop)\n    invalid_trials = ~valid_obs\nif np.any(invalid_trials):\n    fr[invalid_trials] = 0.0\n```\n\niii. The AI notes that `classification == good` in the NWB files represents the classifier-based QC described in the paper (69,943 good units). Invalid neuron-trial pairs are zeroed rather than excluded, preserving the matrix shape.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to the go cue onset. For each trial, the go cue timestamp from `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/go_start_times` is used as the reference time (t=0). Bin edges are computed as offsets from the go cue: `abs_edges = go_times[:, None] + bin_edges_rel[None, :]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo_times_all = h5[\"acquisition\"][\"BehavioralEvents\"][\"go_start_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:n_behavior_trials].astype(np.float64)\n# ...\nabs_edges = go_times[:, None] + bin_edges_rel[None, :]\n```\n\niii. The instructions state \"Temporally align based on Go cue onset\" and the reference code treats spike times as already relative to go cue time. The AI correctly uses go_start_times as the alignment anchor.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The temporal resolution is 50ms (0.05s), producing 80 time bins over the [-2.5s, +1.5s] window. This is a direct binning of raw spike times into non-overlapping 50ms bins -- no temporal rebinning from an intermediate resolution is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH_S = 0.05\nWINDOW_START_S = -2.5\nWINDOW_END_S = 1.5\n\ndef bin_edges_and_centers():\n    edges = np.arange(WINDOW_START_S, WINDOW_END_S + BIN_WIDTH_S * 0.5, BIN_WIDTH_S, dtype=np.float64)\n    centers = edges[:-1] + BIN_WIDTH_S / 2.0\n    return edges, centers\n```\n\niii. The task instructions specify \"Use 50-ms-width bins for computing firing rates.\" The reference code uses 40ms bins with 3.4ms stride, but the AI correctly follows the task instructions rather than the reference preprocessing parameters.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. The tone onset time is derived from `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/sample_start_times/timestamps`, which records the absolute timestamps of sample epoch (tone) onsets. The go cue time from `go_start_times` is used to compute relative timing.\n\nii.\n```python\nsample_start_times = h5[\"acquisition\"][\"BehavioralEvents\"][\"sample_start_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:].astype(np.float64)\n# ...\nsample_onset_abs[trial] = events[0]  # earliest sample_start_times event within trial\nsample_onset_rel_go = sample_onset_abs - go_times\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds*?\n\ni. For each trial, the earliest `sample_start_times` event within the trial window is identified as the tone onset. The tone onset is then expressed relative to the go cue. For each time bin, the input value is `bin_center - sample_onset_rel_go`, giving a continuous time-from-tone-onset value. If no sample event is found within a trial (rare), a fallback of -1.85s relative to go cue is used.\n\nii.\n```python\nEXPECTED_SAMPLE_ONSET_REL_GO = -1.85\n\nfor trial in range(n_trials):\n    events = sample_start_times[sample_slice_starts[trial]:sample_slice_ends[trial]]\n    if len(events):\n        sample_onset_abs[trial] = events[0]\n    else:\n        sample_onset_abs[trial] = go_times[trial] + EXPECTED_SAMPLE_ONSET_REL_GO\n        sample_onset_fallbacks += 1\n\nsample_onset_rel_go = sample_onset_abs - go_times\n\n# Input construction:\ninp[0] = (bin_centers_rel - sample_onset_rel_go[trial]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The AI notes that the expected sample onset relative to go cue is approximately -1.85s based on task timing (3 tones of 150ms + 2 gaps of 100ms = 650ms sample, followed by 1.2s delay). Early-lick trials can have different timing due to epoch replay.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time-from-tone-onset is computed at the same bin centers as the neural data (50ms bins from -2.5s to +1.5s relative to go cue), so it is inherently aligned. Both share the same temporal reference frame (go cue = 0).\n\nii.\n```python\ninp[0] = (bin_centers_rel - sample_onset_rel_go[trial]).astype(np.float32)\n# bin_centers_rel are the same centers used for neural binning\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Photostimulation* derived from?\n\ni. Photostimulation timing is derived from the trial-table columns `intervals/trials/photostim_onset` and `intervals/trials/photostim_duration`. A binary time series is created where bins within `[stim_rel_on, stim_rel_on + duration)` are set to 1, and all others to 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nif str(photostim_onset[trial]) != \"N/A\":\n    stim_rel_on = trial_start[trial] + float(photostim_onset[trial]) - go_times[trial]\n    stim_rel_off = stim_rel_on + float(photostim_duration[trial])\n    inp[1] = ((bin_centers_rel >= stim_rel_on) & (bin_centers_rel < stim_rel_off)).astype(np.float32)\n    photostim_trial_count += 1\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick direction choice* derived from?\n\ni. A hierarchical fallback is used: (1) first post-go-cue lick determines choice (left lick first = 0, right lick first = 1); (2) if no post-go licks, first lick anywhere in the trial is used; (3) if no licks at all (ignore trials), the trial instruction is used (left instruction = 0, right = 1). The choice is encoded as a per-trial scalar broadcast across all time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef infer_choice_for_trial(...) -> tuple[int, str]:\n    left_post = left_lick_times[left_go:left_stop]\n    right_post = right_lick_times[right_go:right_stop]\n    if len(left_post) or len(right_post):\n        first_left = left_post[0] if len(left_post) else np.inf\n        first_right = right_post[0] if len(right_post) else np.inf\n        return (0, \"post_go_lick\") if first_left < first_right else (1, \"post_go_lick\")\n    # ... fallback to any_lick, then instruction_fallback\n    return (0 if instruction == \"left\" else 1, \"instruction_fallback\")\n```\n\niii. The AI notes this fallback is necessary because \"ignore trials have no post-go lick but the decoder spec still requires a binary choice output.\" The encoding matches the instructions: left=0, right=1.\n\n## 6-a. Within each 50ms bin, the last available tongue_y sample is taken (matching the reference code's `align_markers_between_lims` approach). The aligned values are then discretized into three categories using per-session percentiles.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef bin_tongue_y(tongue_timestamps, tongue_y, go_times, bin_edges_rel):\n    abs_edges = go_times[:, None] + bin_edges_rel[None, :]\n    start_idx = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, abs_edges[:, :-1], side=\"left\")\n    end_idx = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, abs_edges[:, 1:], side=\"left\") - 1\n    clipped_end = np.clip(end_idx, 0, len(tongue_y) - 1)\n    valid = end_idx >= start_idx\n    binned = np.full(end_idx.shape, np.nan, dtype=np.float32)\n    binned[valid] = tongue_y[clipped_end[valid]].astype(np.float32)\n    return binned, valid\n```\n\niii.
